Hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for taking the time to see me. I... well, I hope I'm not overreacting by being here. I'm sure you deal with much more serious things. But I suppose what finally got me to make an appointment is that my concentration has just gotten a little... unreliable lately.

Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I know it's probably silly to fret so much, but my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things lately.

But as far as tension specifically from the worrying itself... no, I don't believe I've felt anything like that. I hope that's a good sign? Or... I hope it's okay that I don't have those kinds of symptoms to report. I really want to make sure I'm giving you the right information. Is it normal to just have the racing thoughts without the physical part?

Well, um... I suppose I have been putting a few things off. I mean, I don't want to make it sound like I'm completely falling apart or neglecting my responsibilities, because I'm definitely still working! But... I have had to slow down the retirement handoff a bit.

Well, um... I suppose I do feel a bit on edge a lot of the time, yes. I don't want to overstate it, but... my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things lately.

I suppose I have been putting a few things off. I mean, I don't want to make it sound like I'm completely falling apart or neglecting my responsibilities, because I'm definitely still working! But... I have had to slow down the retirement handoff a bit. \n\nI just... I keep second-guessing all the decisions about the practice, and then I end up sort of losing time at the clinic just sitting there thinking about it all. So I've postponed a few of the final meetings. And, well, even my hobbies at home... my painting and my gardening... they just don't feel as manageable as they used to right now. I just haven't had the energy to start any new projects. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound terribly lazy or incapable, Doctor. I really am trying my best to keep on top of things. Is it... is it understandable that I might be struggling a little bit with getting things done right now?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no obvious reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose I do feel a bit on edge a lot of the time, yes. I don't want to overstate it, but... my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things lately. \n\nIt's mostly about the practice and retiring. I just keep thinking about whether my old patients are being looked after properly by the new folks, and... and whether the financial side of the handoff is completely sound. And then I start worrying about what I'll even do with myself when I'm fully retired—if I'll just become, well, useless, I suppose. \n\nI know it probably sounds a bit silly, and I realize the worry feels a bit out of proportion even to me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I really try my best to push them away. I try to reason with myself, you know, exactly the way I would with a nervous patient sitting in my chair—just telling myself everything is fine, everything is under control. 

But... well, it doesn't always work as well as I'd like. One little concern just sort of... bleeds into the next one. And before I know it, instead of just letting it go like a reasonable person should, I'm back to checking my emails for the fifth time, or reviewing old patient notes, or just asking my colleagues the exact same questions about the transition all over again. 

I know it must be terribly annoying for them, and I really don't want to be a bother to anyone. I just... I can't seem to turn it off once it starts. I don't want to overstate it, but... my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things lately. \n\nIt's mostly about the practice and retiring. I just keep thinking about whether my old patients are being looked after properly by the new folks, and... and whether the financial side of the handoff is completely sound. And then I start worrying about what I'll even do with myself when I'm fully retired—if I'll just become, well, useless, I suppose. \n\nI know it probably sounds a bit silly, and I realize the worry feels a bit out of proportion even to me. I mean, people retire every day and do just fine, right? I really don't want to sound like a complainer who can't handle a normal life transition. Is it... is it okay that I'm feeling this overwhelmed by it all, Doctor? I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill here.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I really try my best to push them away. I try to reason with myself, you know, exactly the way I would with a nervous patient sitting in my chair—just telling myself everything is fine, everything is under control. \n\nBut... well, it doesn't always work as well as I'd like. One little concern just sort of... bleeds into the next one. And before I know it, instead of just letting it go like a reasonable person should, I'm back to checking my emails for the fifth time, or reviewing old patient notes, or just asking my colleagues the exact same questions about the transition all over again. \n\nI know it must be terribly annoying for them, and I really don't want to be a bother to anyone. I just... I can't seem to turn it off once it starts. I hope that doesn't make me sound completely irrational, Doctor? I really am trying to keep it together. Am I making sense when I describe it like that?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel restless or like you're on edge, even when you're not actively worrying about something?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 64 / dentist / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Well, um... I suppose I do feel a bit keyed up sometimes, yes. But I wouldn't say it's all the time, and I really try not to let it show. I mean, I can still sit still in the chair when I'm working with a patient, of course! I'm still perfectly professional. 

But... maybe when I'm at home, sitting in the living room trying to read, I might tap my foot a bit, or feel this sort of... tension in my shoulders. Like I'm waiting for a shoe to drop, even if I can't quite put my finger on what I'm waiting for. 

I really hope that doesn't make me sound too high-strung. I mean, everyone gets a little fidgety sometimes, don't they? Especially at my age, with a big life change coming up. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture, Doctor... does that sound like a normal reaction to you?

Oh, no, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean... I don't think I avoid things because of that. As a dentist, you know, I'm interacting with people all day long, and I've always prided myself on having a good bedside—or chairside—manner. I don't mind people looking at my work or evaluating the practice.

I mean, everyone gets a little fidgety sometimes, don't they? Especially at my age, with a big life change coming up. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture, Doctor... does that sound like a normal reaction to you?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you're afraid of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean... I don't think I avoid things because of that. As a dentist, you know, I'm interacting with people all day long, and I've always prided myself on having a good bedside—or chairside—manner. I don't mind people looking at my work or evaluating the practice. \n\nI suppose I might occasionally shy away from certain social gatherings lately, but that's really just because I'm a bit tired from all the transition planning, not because I'm worried about what people think of me. Oh, goodness, no. Nothing like that. I mean, I have my normal routines, of course—getting the clinic ready in the morning, making sure the instruments are arranged just so. But that's just being thorough, isn't it? Any good dentist does that.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, um... "significant distress" sounds a bit strong. I mean, I'm still showing up for work every day, still taking excellent care of my patients, of course. I wouldn't want you to think I'm falling apart or failing at my job. 

But... I suppose, if I'm being completely honest, it has been getting a little in the way lately. Just a bit. I've actually had to... well, I've had to slow down and postpone some parts of the retirement handoff. I just keep second-guessing all the decisions, you know? And I end up losing time at the clinic just going over the same details again and again to make sure they're perfect. It's a little embarrassing to admit, really, because I'm usually so decisive. 

And even when I'm home, trying to relax... my painting and my gardening, things I usually love and rely on to unwind, they just don't feel quite as manageable as they used to. It feels like a chore to even get started. 

I really hope that doesn't make me sound incompetent, Doctor. It's just such a massive transition, handing over a practice I built from the ground up. It's normal to get a little overwhelmed by that, isn't it? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right impression.

I keep second-guessing all the decisions about the practice, and then I end up sort of losing time at the clinic just sitting there thinking about it all. And then I start worrying about what I'll even do with myself when I'm fully retired—if I'll just become, well, useless, I suppose. \n\nI know it probably sounds a bit silly, and I realize the worry feels a bit out of proportion even to me. So I've postponed a few of the final meetings. And, well, even my hobbies at home... my painting and my gardening... they just don't feel as manageable as they used to right now. I just haven't had the energy to start any new projects. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound terribly lazy or incapable, Doctor. I really am trying my best to keep on top of things. Is it... is it understandable that I might be struggling a little bit with getting things done right now?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no obvious reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose I do feel a bit on edge a lot of the time, yes. I don't want to overstate it, but... my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things lately. \n\nIt's mostly about the practice and retiring. I just keep thinking about whether my old patients are being looked after properly by the new folks, and... and whether the financial side of the handoff is completely sound. And then I start worrying about what I'll even do with myself when I'm fully retired—if I'll just become, well, useless, I suppose. \n\nI know it probably sounds a bit silly, and I realize the worry feels a bit out of proportion even to me. I mean, people retire every day and do just fine, right? I really don't want to sound like a complainer who can't handle a normal life transition. Is it... is it okay that I'm feeling this overwhelmed by it all, Doctor? I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill here.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I really try my best to push them away. I try to reason with myself, you know, exactly the way I would with a nervous patient sitting in my chair—just telling myself everything is fine, everything is under control. \n\nBut... well, it doesn't always work as well as I'd like. One little concern just sort of... bleeds into the next one. And before I know it, instead of just letting it go like a reasonable person should, I'm back to checking my emails for the fifth time, or reviewing old patient notes, or just asking my colleagues the exact same questions about the transition all over again. \n\nI know it must be terribly annoying for them, and I really don't want to be a bother to anyone. I just... I can't seem to turn it off once it starts. I hope that doesn't make me sound completely irrational, Doctor? I really am trying to keep it together. Am I making sense when I describe it like that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you're on edge, even when you're not actively worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose I do feel a bit keyed up sometimes, yes. But I wouldn't say it's all the time, and I really try not to let it show. I mean, I can still sit still in the chair when I'm working with a patient, of course! I'm still perfectly professional. \n\nBut... maybe when I'm at home, sitting in the living room trying to read, I might tap my foot a bit, or feel this sort of... tension in my shoulders. Like I'm waiting for a shoe to drop, even if I can't quite put my finger on what I'm waiting for. \n\nI really hope that doesn't make me sound too high-strung. I mean, everyone gets a little fidgety sometimes, don't they? Especially at my age, with a big life change coming up. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture, Doctor... does that sound like a normal reaction to you?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you're afraid of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean... I don't think I avoid things because of that. As a dentist, you know, I'm interacting with people all day long, and I've always prided myself on having a good bedside—or chairside—manner. I don't mind people looking at my work or evaluating the practice. \n\nI suppose I might occasionally shy away from certain social gatherings lately, but that's really just because I'm a bit tired from all the transition planning, not because I'm worried about what people think of me. I just try to handle things logically, you know? Does that seem reasonable to you, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have your worries or concerns been causing you significant distress or interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... \"significant distress\" sounds a bit strong. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I try not to be too hard on myself. I mean, everyone gets a little stressed, and I think it's important to be forgiving of yourself, right? I wouldn't say I sit around beating myself up over it. 

I suppose... sometimes I do feel a little frustrated that I can't just flip a switch and turn it off. Like I said, I'm usually a very capable person, so feeling a bit... stuck... is unusual for me. But I don't think I'm overly critical. I just try to focus on moving forward and getting things sorted out. 

I hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I'm just trying to be as accurate as I can without making it sound worse than it is.

My mind just sort of wanders off to, well, to everything that could possibly go wrong. It's a bit embarrassing, honestly, to let my mind run away like that at my age. I just wanted to make sure I'm doing the right thing by getting it checked out. It really started... oh, I'd say about a year and three months ago. That's when I formally started the paperwork and planning for my retirement from the practice. It's just a big transition, you know? I'm sure it's completely normal to be a little distracted during a change like that, right? I just hope I haven't let it go on for too long before asking for your advice.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that could go wrong, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I know it's probably silly to fret so much, but my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things lately. \n\nI'll be sitting there, and suddenly I'm worrying about whether my old patients are really being looked after properly by the new partners, or... or whether the financial side of the practice handoff is completely sound. And then, well, I even catch myself worrying about whether I'll just become completely useless once I'm fully retired. \n\nI know it sounds a bit ridiculous when I say it out loud. The worry feels out of proportion, even to me. I try to tell myself everything is fine, but the thoughts just sort of stick there. I hope that doesn't make me sound too neurotic, Doctor. Is it common for people my age to get so wound up over these kinds of things?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or tension in your body when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any headaches. I mean, my shoulders get a little stiff sometimes, but I've always just chalked that up to leaning over dental chairs for the last forty years. I certainly wouldn't want to complain to you about normal, everyday aches and pains. \n\nBut as far as tension specifically from the worrying itself... no, I don't believe I've felt anything like that. I hope that's a good sign? Or... I hope it's okay that I don't have those kinds of symptoms to report. I really want to make sure I'm giving you the right information. Is it normal to just have the racing thoughts without the physical part?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about social situations or being evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say so. Not social situations. I mean, being a dentist, you're constantly chatting with people, trying to make them comfortable in the chair... so I've always been pretty at ease with others. I don't really worry about what people think of me in that way. \n\nI hope that's... well, I hope it's helpful for you that I don't have that particular issue? I certainly don't want to sound overconfident or anything, I just... I really want to make sure I'm giving you what you need. Am I doing okay with answering these questions, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or tasks because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose I have been putting a few things off. I mean, I don't want to make it sound like I'm completely falling apart or neglecting my responsibilities, because I'm definitely still working! But... I have had to slow down the retirement handoff a bit. \n\nI just... I keep second-guessing all the decisions about the practice, and then I end up sort of losing time at the clinic just sitting there thinking about it all. So I've postponed a few of the final meetings. And, well, even my hobbies at home... my painting and my gardening... they just don't feel as manageable as they used to right now. I just haven't had the energy to start any new projects. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound terribly lazy or incapable, Doctor. I really am trying my best to keep on top of things. Is it... is it understandable that I might be struggling a little bit with getting things done right now?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no obvious reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose I do feel a bit on edge a lot of the time, yes. I don't want to overstate it, but... my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things lately. \n\nIt's mostly about the practice and retiring. I just keep thinking about whether my old patients are being looked after properly by the new folks, and... and whether the financial side of the handoff is completely sound. And then I start worrying about what I'll even do with myself when I'm fully retired—if I'll just become, well, useless, I suppose. \n\nI know it probably sounds a bit silly, and I realize the worry feels a bit out of proportion even to me. I mean, people retire every day and do just fine, right? I really don't want to sound like a complainer who can't handle a normal life transition. Is it... is it okay that I'm feeling this overwhelmed by it all, Doctor? I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill here.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I really try my best to push them away. I try to reason with myself, you know, exactly the way I would with a nervous patient sitting in my chair—just telling myself everything is fine, everything is under control. \n\nBut... well, it doesn't always work as well as I'd like. One little concern just sort of... bleeds into the next one. And before I know it, instead of just letting it go like a reasonable person should, I'm back to checking my emails for the fifth time, or reviewing old patient notes, or just asking my colleagues the exact same questions about the transition all over again. \n\nI know it must be terribly annoying for them, and I really don't want to be a bother to anyone. I just... I can't seem to turn it off once it starts. I hope that doesn't make me sound completely irrational, Doctor? I really am trying to keep it together. Am I making sense when I describe it like that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you're on edge, even when you're not actively worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose I do feel a bit keyed up sometimes, yes. But I wouldn't say it's all the time, and I really try not to let it show. I mean, I can still sit still in the chair when I'm working with a patient, of course! I'm still perfectly professional. \n\nBut... maybe when I'm at home, sitting in the living room trying to read, I might tap my foot a bit, or feel this sort of... tension in my shoulders. Like I'm waiting for a shoe to drop, even if I can't quite put my finger on what I'm waiting for. \n\nI really hope that doesn't make me sound too high-strung. I mean, everyone gets a little fidgety sometimes, don't they? Especially at my age, with a big life change coming up. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture, Doctor... does that sound like a normal reaction to you?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you're afraid of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean... I don't think I avoid things because of that. As a dentist, you know, I'm interacting with people all day long, and I've always prided myself on having a good bedside—or chairside—manner. I don't mind people looking at my work or evaluating the practice. \n\nI suppose I might occasionally shy away from certain social gatherings lately, but that's really just because I'm a bit tired from all the transition planning, not because I'm worried about what people think of me. I mean, I've lived in this community a long time, people know me. I wouldn't want you to think I'm suddenly becoming a recluse or anything like that. I'm still very capable socially. Does that... does that answer your question, Doctor? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or routines in order to alleviate your worries?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Nothing like that. I mean, I have my normal routines, of course—getting the clinic ready in the morning, making sure the instruments are arranged just so. But that's just being thorough, isn't it? Any good dentist does that. \n\nI don't... I don't have to tap the door frame three times or wash my hands a certain number of times to feel okay, if that's what you mean. I know some people struggle with those sorts of things, but I've never felt the urge to do anything out of the ordinary to calm myself down. Just... just the usual double-checking of the transition paperwork, like I mentioned before. \n\nI hope I'm understanding your question correctly? I wouldn't want to mislead you. I just try to handle things logically, you know? Does that seem reasonable to you, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have your worries or concerns been causing you significant distress or interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... \"significant distress\" sounds a bit strong. I mean, I'm still showing up for work every day, still taking excellent care of my patients, of course. I wouldn't want you to think I'm falling apart or failing at my job. \n\nBut... I suppose, if I'm being completely honest, it has been getting a little in the way lately. Just a bit. I've actually had to... well, I've had to slow down and postpone some parts of the retirement handoff. I just keep second-guessing all the decisions, you know? And I end up losing time at the clinic just going over the same details again and again to make sure they're perfect. It's a little embarrassing to admit, really, because I'm usually so decisive. \n\nAnd even when I'm home, trying to relax... my painting and my gardening, things I usually love and rely on to unwind, they just don't feel quite as manageable as they used to. It feels like a chore to even get started. \n\nI really hope that doesn't make me sound incompetent, Doctor. It's just such a massive transition, handing over a practice I built from the ground up. It's normal to get a little overwhelmed by that, isn't it? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right impression.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about one area of your life, like your retirement and the dental practice, or do they cover a wide range of topics?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, the retirement is certainly the biggest piece of it, naturally. Anyone would be focused on that, right? But... if I'm really thinking about it, it's not just the handoff itself. \n\nIt's... well, my mind just sort of keeps circling around all sorts of things lately. I worry constantly about whether my old patients are going to be looked after properly by the new team. And then I start fretting over whether the financial side of the handoff is completely sound, even though my accountant says it's fine. And then... well, I even catch myself worrying about what I'll do with myself. Whether I'll just become completely useless once I don't have the clinic to go to every day. \n\nIt sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, doesn't it? I know it's probably out of proportion to what's actually happening, even to me. I just... I want to make sure everything is taken care of. Does that make sense, Doctor? I'm not trying to make a mountain out of a molehill, I just want to give you the full picture.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself for worrying too much or for not being able to control your worries?\n\nPatient: Well, I try not to be too hard on myself. I mean, everyone gets a little stressed, and I think it's important to be forgiving of yourself, right? Like I said, I'm usually a very capable person, so feeling a bit... stuck... is unusual for me. But I don't think I'm overly critical. I just try to focus on moving forward and getting things sorted out. \n\nI hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? It really started... oh, I'd say about a year and three months ago. That's when I formally started the paperwork and planning for my retirement from the practice. It's just a big transition, you know? I'm sure it's completely normal to be a little distracted during a change like that, right? I just hope I haven't let it go on for too long before asking for your advice.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that could go wrong, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I know it's probably silly to fret so much, but my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things lately. \n\nI'll be sitting there, and suddenly I'm worrying about whether my old patients are really being looked after properly by the new partners, or... or whether the financial side of the practice handoff is completely sound. And then, well, I even catch myself worrying about whether I'll just become completely useless once I'm fully retired. \n\nI know it sounds a bit ridiculous when I say it out loud. The worry feels out of proportion, even to me. I try to tell myself everything is fine, but the thoughts just sort of stick there. I hope that doesn't make me sound too neurotic, Doctor. Is it common for people my age to get so wound up over these kinds of things?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or tension in your body when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any headaches. I mean, my shoulders get a little stiff sometimes, but I've always just chalked that up to leaning over dental chairs for the last forty years. I certainly wouldn't want to complain to you about normal, everyday aches and pains. \n\nBut as far as tension specifically from the worrying itself... no, I don't believe I've felt anything like that. I hope that's a good sign? Or... I hope it's okay that I don't have those kinds of symptoms to report. I really want to make sure I'm giving you the right information. Is it normal to just have the racing thoughts without the physical part?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about social situations or being evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say so. Not social situations. I mean, being a dentist, you're constantly chatting with people, trying to make them comfortable in the chair... so I've always been pretty at ease with others. I don't really worry about what people think of me in that way. \n\nI hope that's... well, I hope it's helpful for you that I don't have that particular issue? I certainly don't want to sound overconfident or anything, I just... I really want to make sure I'm giving you what you need. Am I doing okay with answering these questions, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or tasks because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose I have been putting a few things off. I mean, I don't want to make it sound like I'm completely falling apart or neglecting my responsibilities, because I'm definitely still working! But... I have had to slow down the retirement handoff a bit. \n\nI just... I keep second-guessing all the decisions about the practice, and then I end up sort of losing time at the clinic just sitting there thinking about it all. So I've postponed a few of the final meetings. And, well, even my hobbies at home... my painting and my gardening... they just don't feel as manageable as they used to right now. I just haven't had the energy to start any new projects. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound terribly lazy or incapable, Doctor. I really am trying my best to keep on top of things. Is it... is it understandable that I might be struggling a little bit with getting things done right now?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no obvious reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Well, um... And then I start worrying about what I'll even do with myself when I'm fully retired—if I'll just become, well, useless, I suppose. \n\nI know it probably sounds a bit silly, and I realize the worry feels a bit out of proportion even to me. Is it... is it okay that I'm feeling this overwhelmed by it all, Doctor? I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill here.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I really try my best to push them away. I try to reason with myself, you know, exactly the way I would with a nervous patient sitting in my chair—just telling myself everything is fine, everything is under control. \n\nBut... well, it doesn't always work as well as I'd like. One little concern just sort of... bleeds into the next one. And before I know it, instead of just letting it go like a reasonable person should, I'm back to checking my emails for the fifth time, or reviewing old patient notes, or just asking my colleagues the exact same questions about the transition all over again. \n\nI know it must be terribly annoying for them, and I really don't want to be a bother to anyone. I just... I can't seem to turn it off once it starts. I hope that doesn't make me sound completely irrational, Doctor? I really am trying to keep it together. I mean, I can still sit still in the chair when I'm working with a patient, of course! I'm still perfectly professional. \n\nBut... maybe when I'm at home, sitting in the living room trying to read, I might tap my foot a bit, or feel this sort of... tension in my shoulders. Like I'm waiting for a shoe to drop, even if I can't quite put my finger on what I'm waiting for. \n\nI really hope that doesn't make me sound too high-strung. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture, Doctor... does that sound like a normal reaction to you?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you're afraid of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean... I don't think I avoid things because of that. As a dentist, you know, I'm interacting with people all day long, and I've always prided myself on having a good bedside—or chairside—manner. I don't mind people looking at my work or evaluating the practice. \n\nI suppose I might occasionally shy away from certain social gatherings lately, but that's really just because I'm a bit tired from all the transition planning, not because I'm worried about what people think of me. Does that... does that answer your question, Doctor? Well, I wouldn't call them intrusive, exactly. That sounds a bit... extreme, doesn't it? I mean, I'm not losing my mind or anything like that. It's just that, well, I'm a thorough person. As a dentist, you have to be.

I hope that's a good sign? Or... I hope it's okay that I don't have those kinds of symptoms to report. I really want to make sure I'm giving you the right information. Is it normal to just have the racing thoughts without the physical part?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about social situations or being evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say so. Not social situations. I mean, being a dentist, you're constantly chatting with people, trying to make them comfortable in the chair... so I've always been pretty at ease with others. I don't really worry about what people think of me in that way. \n\nI hope that's... well, I hope it's helpful for you that I don't have that particular issue? I certainly don't want to sound overconfident or anything, I just... I really want to make sure I'm giving you what you need. Am I doing okay with answering these questions, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or tasks because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose I have been putting a few things off. I mean, I don't want to make it sound like I'm completely falling apart or neglecting my responsibilities, because I'm definitely still working! But... I have had to slow down the retirement handoff a bit. \n\nI just... I keep second-guessing all the decisions about the practice, and then I end up sort of losing time at the clinic just sitting there thinking about it all. So I've postponed a few of the final meetings. And, well, even my hobbies at home... my painting and my gardening... they just don't feel as manageable as they used to right now. I just haven't had the energy to start any new projects. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound terribly lazy or incapable, Doctor. I really am trying my best to keep on top of things. Is it... is it understandable that I might be struggling a little bit with getting things done right now?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no obvious reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose I do feel a bit on edge a lot of the time, yes. I don't want to overstate it, but... my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things lately. \n\nIt's mostly about the practice and retiring. I just keep thinking about whether my old patients are being looked after properly by the new folks, and... and whether the financial side of the handoff is completely sound. And then I start worrying about what I'll even do with myself when I'm fully retired—if I'll just become, well, useless, I suppose. \n\nI know it probably sounds a bit silly, and I realize the worry feels a bit out of proportion even to me. I mean, people retire every day and do just fine, right? I really don't want to sound like a complainer who can't handle a normal life transition. Is it... is it okay that I'm feeling this overwhelmed by it all, Doctor? I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill here.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I really try my best to push them away. I try to reason with myself, you know, exactly the way I would with a nervous patient sitting in my chair—just telling myself everything is fine, everything is under control. \n\nBut... well, it doesn't always work as well as I'd like. One little concern just sort of... bleeds into the next one. And before I know it, instead of just letting it go like a reasonable person should, I'm back to checking my emails for the fifth time, or reviewing old patient notes, or just asking my colleagues the exact same questions about the transition all over again. \n\nI know it must be terribly annoying for them, and I really don't want to be a bother to anyone. I just... I can't seem to turn it off once it starts. I hope that doesn't make me sound completely irrational, Doctor? I really am trying to keep it together. Am I making sense when I describe it like that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you're on edge, even when you're not actively worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose I do feel a bit keyed up sometimes, yes. But I wouldn't say it's all the time, and I really try not to let it show. I mean, I can still sit still in the chair when I'm working with a patient, of course! I'm still perfectly professional. \n\nBut... maybe when I'm at home, sitting in the living room trying to read, I might tap my foot a bit, or feel this sort of... tension in my shoulders. Like I'm waiting for a shoe to drop, even if I can't quite put my finger on what I'm waiting for. \n\nI really hope that doesn't make me sound too high-strung. I mean, everyone gets a little fidgety sometimes, don't they? Especially at my age, with a big life change coming up. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture, Doctor... does that sound like a normal reaction to you?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you're afraid of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean... I don't think I avoid things because of that. As a dentist, you know, I'm interacting with people all day long, and I've always prided myself on having a good bedside—or chairside—manner. I don't mind people looking at my work or evaluating the practice. \n\nI suppose I might occasionally shy away from certain social gatherings lately, but that's really just because I'm a bit tired from all the transition planning, not because I'm worried about what people think of me. I mean, I've lived in this community a long time, people know me. I wouldn't want you to think I'm suddenly becoming a recluse or anything like that. I'm still very capable socially. Does that... does that answer your question, Doctor? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or routines in order to alleviate your worries?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Nothing like that. I mean, I have my normal routines, of course—getting the clinic ready in the morning, making sure the instruments are arranged just so. But that's just being thorough, isn't it? Any good dentist does that. \n\nI don't... I don't have to tap the door frame three times or wash my hands a certain number of times to feel okay, if that's what you mean. I know some people struggle with those sorts of things, but I've never felt the urge to do anything out of the ordinary to calm myself down. Just... just the usual double-checking of the transition paperwork, like I mentioned before. \n\nI hope I'm understanding your question correctly? I wouldn't want to mislead you. I just try to handle things logically, you know? Does that seem reasonable to you, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have your worries or concerns been causing you significant distress or interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... \"significant distress\" sounds a bit strong. I mean, I'm still showing up for work every day, still taking excellent care of my patients, of course. I wouldn't want you to think I'm falling apart or failing at my job. \n\nBut... I suppose, if I'm being completely honest, it has been getting a little in the way lately. Just a bit. I've actually had to... well, I've had to slow down and postpone some parts of the retirement handoff. I just keep second-guessing all the decisions, you know? And I end up losing time at the clinic just going over the same details again and again to make sure they're perfect. It's a little embarrassing to admit, really, because I'm usually so decisive. \n\nAnd even when I'm home, trying to relax... my painting and my gardening, things I usually love and rely on to unwind, they just don't feel quite as manageable as they used to. It feels like a chore to even get started. \n\nI really hope that doesn't make me sound incompetent, Doctor. It's just such a massive transition, handing over a practice I built from the ground up. It's normal to get a little overwhelmed by that, isn't it? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right impression.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about one area of your life, like your retirement and the dental practice, or do they cover a wide range of topics?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, the retirement is certainly the biggest piece of it, naturally. Anyone would be focused on that, right? But... if I'm really thinking about it, it's not just the handoff itself. \n\nIt's... well, my mind just sort of keeps circling around all sorts of things lately. I worry constantly about whether my old patients are going to be looked after properly by the new team. And then I start fretting over whether the financial side of the handoff is completely sound, even though my accountant says it's fine. And then... well, I even catch myself worrying about what I'll do with myself. Whether I'll just become completely useless once I don't have the clinic to go to every day. \n\nIt sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, doesn't it? I know it's probably out of proportion to what's actually happening, even to me. I just... I want to make sure everything is taken care of. Does that make sense, Doctor? I'm not trying to make a mountain out of a molehill, I just want to give you the full picture.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself for worrying too much or for not being able to control your worries?\n\nPatient: Well, I try not to be too hard on myself. I mean, everyone gets a little stressed, and I think it's important to be forgiving of yourself, right? I wouldn't say I sit around beating myself up over it. \n\nI suppose... sometimes I do feel a little frustrated that I can't just flip a switch and turn it off. Like I said, I'm usually a very capable person, so feeling a bit... stuck... is unusual for me. But I don't think I'm overly critical. I just try to focus on moving forward and getting things sorted out. \n\nI hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I'm just trying to be as accurate as I can without making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your relationships with friends or family due to your worries about retirement and the dental practice?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, no, absolutely not. I haven't taken any full days off. I'm very dedicated to my patients, and I'd hate to let anyone down or leave my staff in a bind. I've always prided myself on a strong work ethic, you know? 

I will admit, though... well, I suppose I have had to slow down a little bit with the actual transition. I've postponed a few parts of the retirement handoff. It's just that I keep second-guessing my decisions—wanting to make sure everything is perfectly set up for the new owners—and I end up losing a bit of time at the clinic just double-checking things instead of moving forward. Nothing major, of course, just being careful. 

Though I suppose it does take it out of me a little more than I'd like to admit. Even my hobbies... I usually love my painting and my gardening on the weekends, but lately, they just don't feel quite as manageable as they used to. I just haven't quite had the focus for them. 

But I haven't missed a single day of work! That shows I'm keeping it all together fairly well, doesn't it, Doctor? I hope you can see I'm really trying my best to handle it all responsibly.

It's just a big transition, you know? I'm sure it's completely normal to be a little distracted during a change like that, right? I just hope I haven't let it go on for too long before asking for your advice.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that could go wrong, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I know it's probably silly to fret so much, but my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things lately. \n\nI'll be sitting there, and suddenly I'm worrying about whether my old patients are really being looked after properly by the new partners, or... or whether the financial side of the practice handoff is completely sound. And then, well, I even catch myself worrying about whether I'll just become completely useless once I'm fully retired. \n\nI know it sounds a bit ridiculous when I say it out loud. The worry feels out of proportion, even to me. I try to tell myself everything is fine, but the thoughts just sort of stick there. I hope that doesn't make me sound too neurotic, Doctor. Is it common for people my age to get so wound up over these kinds of things?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or tension in your body when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any headaches. I mean, my shoulders get a little stiff sometimes, but I've always just chalked that up to leaning over dental chairs for the last forty years. I certainly wouldn't want to complain to you about normal, everyday aches and pains. \n\nBut as far as tension specifically from the worrying itself... no, I don't believe I've felt anything like that. I hope that's a good sign? Or... I hope it's okay that I don't have those kinds of symptoms to report. I really want to make sure I'm giving you the right information. Is it normal to just have the racing thoughts without the physical part?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about social situations or being evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say so. Not social situations. I mean, being a dentist, you're constantly chatting with people, trying to make them comfortable in the chair... so I've always been pretty at ease with others. I don't really worry about what people think of me in that way. \n\nI hope that's... well, I hope it's helpful for you that I don't have that particular issue? I certainly don't want to sound overconfident or anything, I just... I really want to make sure I'm giving you what you need. Am I doing okay with answering these questions, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or tasks because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I have had to slow down the retirement handoff a bit. \n\nI just... I keep second-guessing all the decisions about the practice, and then I end up sort of losing time at the clinic just sitting there thinking about it all. So I've postponed a few of the final meetings. And, well, even my hobbies at home... my painting and my gardening... they just don't feel as manageable as they used to right now. I just haven't had the energy to start any new projects. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound terribly lazy or incapable, Doctor. I really am trying my best to keep on top of things. I just... I can't seem to turn it off once it starts. I hope that doesn't make me sound completely irrational, Doctor? I really am trying to keep it together.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.8, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, Doctor. Thank you so much for taking the time to see me. I... well, I hope I'm not overreacting by being here. I'm sure you deal with much more serious things. But I suppose what finally got me to make an appointment is that my concentration has just gotten a little... unreliable lately. \n\nI'm a dentist, and I'll be reviewing a patient's chart at the clinic, or even just at home trying to relax and lay out a watercolor wash, and suddenly I realize I've read the same line three times. My mind just sort of wanders off to, well, to everything that could possibly go wrong. It's a bit embarrassing, honestly, to let my mind run away like that at my age. I just wanted to make sure I'm doing the right thing by getting it checked out. Is that... does that sound like an okay reason to come in?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this problem with your concentration?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it hasn't been forever. It really started... oh, I'd say about a year and three months ago. That's when I formally started the paperwork and planning for my retirement from the practice. It's just a big transition, you know? I'm sure it's completely normal to be a little distracted during a change like that, right? I just hope I haven't let it go on for too long before asking for your advice.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying about things that could go wrong, even when there's no apparent reason to worry?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I know it's probably silly to fret so much, but my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things lately. \n\nI'll be sitting there, and suddenly I'm worrying about whether my old patients are really being looked after properly by the new partners, or... or whether the financial side of the practice handoff is completely sound. And then, well, I even catch myself worrying about whether I'll just become completely useless once I'm fully retired. \n\nI know it sounds a bit ridiculous when I say it out loud. The worry feels out of proportion, even to me. I try to tell myself everything is fine, but the thoughts just sort of stick there. I hope that doesn't make me sound too neurotic, Doctor. Is it common for people my age to get so wound up over these kinds of things?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any physical symptoms like headaches or tension in your body when you're worrying?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... no, I don't think so. I haven't really noticed any headaches. I mean, my shoulders get a little stiff sometimes, but I've always just chalked that up to leaning over dental chairs for the last forty years. I certainly wouldn't want to complain to you about normal, everyday aches and pains. \n\nBut as far as tension specifically from the worrying itself... no, I don't believe I've felt anything like that. I hope that's a good sign? Or... I hope it's okay that I don't have those kinds of symptoms to report. I really want to make sure I'm giving you the right information. Is it normal to just have the racing thoughts without the physical part?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about social situations or being evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say so. Not social situations. I mean, being a dentist, you're constantly chatting with people, trying to make them comfortable in the chair... so I've always been pretty at ease with others. I don't really worry about what people think of me in that way. \n\nI hope that's... well, I hope it's helpful for you that I don't have that particular issue? I certainly don't want to sound overconfident or anything, I just... I really want to make sure I'm giving you what you need. Am I doing okay with answering these questions, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any activities or tasks because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose I have been putting a few things off. I mean, I don't want to make it sound like I'm completely falling apart or neglecting my responsibilities, because I'm definitely still working! But... I have had to slow down the retirement handoff a bit. \n\nI just... I keep second-guessing all the decisions about the practice, and then I end up sort of losing time at the clinic just sitting there thinking about it all. So I've postponed a few of the final meetings. And, well, even my hobbies at home... my painting and my gardening... they just don't feel as manageable as they used to right now. I just haven't had the energy to start any new projects. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound terribly lazy or incapable, Doctor. I really am trying my best to keep on top of things. Is it... is it understandable that I might be struggling a little bit with getting things done right now?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious most of the time, even when there's no obvious reason to be anxious?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose I do feel a bit on edge a lot of the time, yes. I don't want to overstate it, but... my mind just keeps circling around all sorts of things lately. \n\nIt's mostly about the practice and retiring. I just keep thinking about whether my old patients are being looked after properly by the new folks, and... and whether the financial side of the handoff is completely sound. And then I start worrying about what I'll even do with myself when I'm fully retired—if I'll just become, well, useless, I suppose. \n\nI know it probably sounds a bit silly, and I realize the worry feels a bit out of proportion even to me. I mean, people retire every day and do just fine, right? I really don't want to sound like a complainer who can't handle a normal life transition. Is it... is it okay that I'm feeling this overwhelmed by it all, Doctor? I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill here.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I really try my best to push them away. I try to reason with myself, you know, exactly the way I would with a nervous patient sitting in my chair—just telling myself everything is fine, everything is under control. \n\nBut... well, it doesn't always work as well as I'd like. One little concern just sort of... bleeds into the next one. And before I know it, instead of just letting it go like a reasonable person should, I'm back to checking my emails for the fifth time, or reviewing old patient notes, or just asking my colleagues the exact same questions about the transition all over again. \n\nI know it must be terribly annoying for them, and I really don't want to be a bother to anyone. I just... I can't seem to turn it off once it starts. I hope that doesn't make me sound completely irrational, Doctor? I really am trying to keep it together. Am I making sense when I describe it like that?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or like you're on edge, even when you're not actively worrying about something?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I suppose I do feel a bit keyed up sometimes, yes. But I wouldn't say it's all the time, and I really try not to let it show. I mean, I can still sit still in the chair when I'm working with a patient, of course! I'm still perfectly professional. \n\nBut... maybe when I'm at home, sitting in the living room trying to read, I might tap my foot a bit, or feel this sort of... tension in my shoulders. Like I'm waiting for a shoe to drop, even if I can't quite put my finger on what I'm waiting for. \n\nI really hope that doesn't make me sound too high-strung. I mean, everyone gets a little fidgety sometimes, don't they? Especially at my age, with a big life change coming up. I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture, Doctor... does that sound like a normal reaction to you?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you're afraid of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean... I don't think I avoid things because of that. As a dentist, you know, I'm interacting with people all day long, and I've always prided myself on having a good bedside—or chairside—manner. I don't mind people looking at my work or evaluating the practice. \n\nI suppose I might occasionally shy away from certain social gatherings lately, but that's really just because I'm a bit tired from all the transition planning, not because I'm worried about what people think of me. I mean, I've lived in this community a long time, people know me. I wouldn't want you to think I'm suddenly becoming a recluse or anything like that. I'm still very capable socially. Does that... does that answer your question, Doctor? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or routines in order to alleviate your worries?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Nothing like that. I mean, I have my normal routines, of course—getting the clinic ready in the morning, making sure the instruments are arranged just so. But that's just being thorough, isn't it? Any good dentist does that. \n\nI don't... I don't have to tap the door frame three times or wash my hands a certain number of times to feel okay, if that's what you mean. I know some people struggle with those sorts of things, but I've never felt the urge to do anything out of the ordinary to calm myself down. Just... just the usual double-checking of the transition paperwork, like I mentioned before. \n\nI hope I'm understanding your question correctly? I wouldn't want to mislead you. I just try to handle things logically, you know? Does that seem reasonable to you, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have your worries or concerns been causing you significant distress or interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... \"significant distress\" sounds a bit strong. I mean, I'm still showing up for work every day, still taking excellent care of my patients, of course. I wouldn't want you to think I'm falling apart or failing at my job. \n\nBut... I suppose, if I'm being completely honest, it has been getting a little in the way lately. Just a bit. I've actually had to... well, I've had to slow down and postpone some parts of the retirement handoff. I just keep second-guessing all the decisions, you know? And I end up losing time at the clinic just going over the same details again and again to make sure they're perfect. It's a little embarrassing to admit, really, because I'm usually so decisive. \n\nAnd even when I'm home, trying to relax... my painting and my gardening, things I usually love and rely on to unwind, they just don't feel quite as manageable as they used to. It feels like a chore to even get started. \n\nI really hope that doesn't make me sound incompetent, Doctor. It's just such a massive transition, handing over a practice I built from the ground up. It's normal to get a little overwhelmed by that, isn't it? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right impression.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about one area of your life, like your retirement and the dental practice, or do they cover a wide range of topics?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, the retirement is certainly the biggest piece of it, naturally. Anyone would be focused on that, right? But... if I'm really thinking about it, it's not just the handoff itself. \n\nIt's... well, my mind just sort of keeps circling around all sorts of things lately. I worry constantly about whether my old patients are going to be looked after properly by the new team. And then I start fretting over whether the financial side of the handoff is completely sound, even though my accountant says it's fine. And then... well, I even catch myself worrying about what I'll do with myself. Whether I'll just become completely useless once I don't have the clinic to go to every day. \n\nIt sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, doesn't it? I know it's probably out of proportion to what's actually happening, even to me. I just... I want to make sure everything is taken care of. Does that make sense, Doctor? I'm not trying to make a mountain out of a molehill, I just want to give you the full picture.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself for worrying too much or for not being able to control your worries?\n\nPatient: Well, I try not to be too hard on myself. I mean, everyone gets a little stressed, and I think it's important to be forgiving of yourself, right? I wouldn't say I sit around beating myself up over it. \n\nI suppose... sometimes I do feel a little frustrated that I can't just flip a switch and turn it off. Like I said, I'm usually a very capable person, so feeling a bit... stuck... is unusual for me. But I don't think I'm overly critical. I just try to focus on moving forward and getting things sorted out. \n\nI hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I'm just trying to be as accurate as I can without making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your relationships with friends or family due to your worries about retirement and the dental practice?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I mean... my wife and my children have been incredibly supportive through all this. I really make it a point not to let my preoccupations spill over into our time together. \n\nI suppose I might be just a tiny bit quieter at dinner sometimes, you know, just lost in thought about the clinic, but I always try to be present. I'd absolutely hate to think I was burdening them with my own stress. That wouldn't be very fair to them at all, would it? \n\nSo, no, I haven't noticed any real problems there. My friendships and my marriage are perfectly fine. I try very hard to keep things pleasant for everyone around me. That's a good sign, isn't it, Doctor? I hope that shows I'm still managing things reasonably well.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are repetitive, unwanted, and intrusive, making it difficult for you to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't call them intrusive, exactly. That sounds a bit... extreme, doesn't it? I mean, I'm not losing my mind or anything like that. It's just that, well, I'm a thorough person. As a dentist, you have to be. \n\nBut I suppose... yes, sometimes a thought does kind of get stuck. I try to reason with myself, you know, exactly the way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. I tell myself everything is fine, the handoff is on track. But then one little concern just sort of... leads to the next. And before I know it, instead of just letting it go and enjoying my evening, I'm back on the computer checking emails from the brokers, or reviewing my clinic notes, or... or asking my wife the same questions about our retirement budget again. \n\nIt's just me being diligent, really. I just want to be absolutely sure everything is in order. That's a perfectly normal way to handle a big transition, wouldn't you agree, Doctor? I'm sure you see a lot of professionals who just like to be thorough.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take any days off from work due to your worries about the retirement and the dental practice?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, absolutely not. I haven't taken any full days off. I'm very dedicated to my patients, and I'd hate to let anyone down or leave my staff in a bind. I've always prided myself on a strong work ethic, you know? \n\nI will admit, though... well, I suppose I have had to slow down a little bit with the actual transition. I've postponed a few parts of the retirement handoff. It's just that I keep second-guessing my decisions—wanting to make sure everything is perfectly set up for the new owners—and I end up losing a bit of time at the clinic just double-checking things instead of moving forward. Nothing major, of course, just being careful. \n\nThough I suppose it does take it out of me a little more than I'd like to admit. Even my hobbies... I usually love my painting and my gardening on the weekends, but lately, they just don't feel quite as manageable as they used to. I just haven't quite had the focus for them. \n\nBut I haven't missed a single day of work! That shows I'm keeping it all together fairly well, doesn't it, Doctor? I hope you can see I'm really trying my best to handle it all responsibly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your thoughts about the retirement and dental practice, even when you try to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't say \"unable to control.\" That sounds like I'm completely falling apart, and I assure you, I'm not. I'm just... very invested in making sure this transition is seamless. \n\nBut, um, as I think I mentioned a moment ago... I do try very hard to just put it out of my mind. I try to reason with myself, exactly the way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. I tell myself, \"You've done the prep work, everything is fine.\" \n\nBut then... well, one little concern just sort of leads to the next, doesn't it? I try to think about something else, maybe watch a bit of television, but then I'm wondering if I filed that one specific piece of paperwork for the broker. And before I know it, instead of just letting it go, I'm back on the computer checking emails, or reviewing my clinic notes, or... asking my poor wife the same questions about our budget again. \n\nI just want to be certain, you know? It's just a sign of being conscientious, wouldn't you say? I really hope you don't think I'm letting this get completely out of hand, Doctor. I'm just trying to be responsible.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I just try to focus on moving forward and getting things sorted out. \n\nI hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I'm just trying to be as accurate as I can without making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your relationships with friends or family due to your worries about retirement and the dental practice?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. I mean... my wife and my children have been incredibly supportive through all this. I really make it a point not to let my preoccupations spill over into our time together. \n\nI suppose I might be just a tiny bit quieter at dinner sometimes, you know, just lost in thought about the clinic, but I always try to be present. I'd absolutely hate to think I was burdening them with my own stress. That wouldn't be very fair to them at all, would it? \n\nSo, no, I haven't noticed any real problems there. My friendships and my marriage are perfectly fine. I try very hard to keep things pleasant for everyone around me. That's a good sign, isn't it, Doctor? I hope that shows I'm still managing things reasonably well.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that are repetitive, unwanted, and intrusive, making it difficult for you to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Well, I wouldn't call them intrusive, exactly. That sounds a bit... extreme, doesn't it? I mean, I'm not losing my mind or anything like that. It's just that, well, I'm a thorough person. As a dentist, you have to be. \n\nBut I suppose... yes, sometimes a thought does kind of get stuck. I try to reason with myself, you know, exactly the way I would with a nervous patient in the chair. I tell myself everything is fine, the handoff is on track. But then one little concern just sort of... leads to the next. I just want to be absolutely sure everything is in order. That's a perfectly normal way to handle a big transition, wouldn't you agree, Doctor? I'm sure you see a lot of professionals who just like to be thorough.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to take any days off from work due to your worries about the retirement and the dental practice?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, absolutely not. I haven't taken any full days off. I'm very dedicated to my patients, and I'd hate to let anyone down or leave my staff in a bind. I've always prided myself on a strong work ethic, you know? \n\nI will admit, though... well, I suppose I have had to slow down a little bit with the actual transition. I've postponed a few parts of the retirement handoff. Well, um... as I think I touched on a little earlier, I wouldn't use a strong word like struggle. That sounds so... incompetent, and I really do pride myself on my competence. I'd hate for you to think I'm dropping the ball or failing my staff.

I hope I'm not making a mountain out of a molehill here.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to ignore them?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I do. I mean, I really try my best to push them away. I try to reason with myself, you know, exactly the way I would with a nervous patient sitting in my chair—just telling myself everything is fine, everything is under control. \n\nBut... well, it doesn't always work as well as I'd like. One little concern just sort of... bleeds into the next one. And before I know it, instead of just letting it go like a reasonable person should, I'm back to checking my emails for the fifth time, or reviewing old patient notes, or just asking my colleagues the exact same questions about the transition all over again. \n\nI know it must be terribly annoying for them, and I really don't want to be a bother to anyone. I just... I can't seem to turn it off once it starts. I hope that doesn't make me sound completely irrational, Doctor? I really am trying to keep it together. I mean, I can still sit still in the chair when I'm working with a patient, of course! I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right picture, Doctor... does that sound like a normal reaction to you?\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid certain situations because you're afraid of being judged or evaluated by others?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, no, I wouldn't say that. I mean... I don't think I avoid things because of that. As a dentist, you know, I'm interacting with people all day long, and I've always prided myself on having a good bedside—or chairside—manner. I don't mind people looking at my work or evaluating the practice. \n\nI suppose I might occasionally shy away from certain social gatherings lately, but that's really just because I'm a bit tired from all the transition planning, not because I'm worried about what people think of me. I mean, I've lived in this community a long time, people know me. I wouldn't want you to think I'm suddenly becoming a recluse or anything like that. I'm still very capable socially. Does that... does that answer your question, Doctor? I just want to be sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or routines in order to alleviate your worries?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, no. Nothing like that. I mean, I have my normal routines, of course—getting the clinic ready in the morning, making sure the instruments are arranged just so. But that's just being thorough, isn't it? Any good dentist does that. \n\nI don't... I don't have to tap the door frame three times or wash my hands a certain number of times to feel okay, if that's what you mean. I know some people struggle with those sorts of things, but I've never felt the urge to do anything out of the ordinary to calm myself down. Just... just the usual double-checking of the transition paperwork, like I mentioned before. \n\nI hope I'm understanding your question correctly? I wouldn't want to mislead you. I just try to handle things logically, you know? Does that seem reasonable to you, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: Have your worries or concerns been causing you significant distress or interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, um... \"significant distress\" sounds a bit strong. I mean, I'm still showing up for work every day, still taking excellent care of my patients, of course. I wouldn't want you to think I'm falling apart or failing at my job. \n\nBut... I suppose, if I'm being completely honest, it has been getting a little in the way lately. Just a bit. I've actually had to... well, I've had to slow down and postpone some parts of the retirement handoff. I just keep second-guessing all the decisions, you know? And I end up losing time at the clinic just going over the same details again and again to make sure they're perfect. It's a little embarrassing to admit, really, because I'm usually so decisive. \n\nAnd even when I'm home, trying to relax... my painting and my gardening, things I usually love and rely on to unwind, they just don't feel quite as manageable as they used to. It feels like a chore to even get started. \n\nI really hope that doesn't make me sound incompetent, Doctor. It's just such a massive transition, handing over a practice I built from the ground up. It's normal to get a little overwhelmed by that, isn't it? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right impression.\n\nDoctor: Are your worries mostly about one area of your life, like your retirement and the dental practice, or do they cover a wide range of topics?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose... I mean, the retirement is certainly the biggest piece of it, naturally. Anyone would be focused on that, right? But... if I'm really thinking about it, it's not just the handoff itself. \n\nIt's... well, my mind just sort of keeps circling around all sorts of things lately. I worry constantly about whether my old patients are going to be looked after properly by the new team. And then I start fretting over whether the financial side of the handoff is completely sound, even though my accountant says it's fine. And then... well, I even catch myself worrying about what I'll do with myself. Whether I'll just become completely useless once I don't have the clinic to go to every day. \n\nIt sounds a bit silly when I say it out loud, doesn't it? I know it's probably out of proportion to what's actually happening, even to me. I just... I want to make sure everything is taken care of. Does that make sense, Doctor? I'm not trying to make a mountain out of a molehill, I just want to give you the full picture.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself for worrying too much or for not being able to control your worries?\n\nPatient: Well, I try not to be too hard on myself. I mean, everyone gets a little stressed, and I think it's important to be forgiving of yourself, right? I wouldn't say I sit around beating myself up over it. \n\nI suppose... sometimes I do feel a little frustrated that I can't just flip a switch and turn it off. Like I said, I'm usually a very capable person, so feeling a bit... stuck... is unusual for me. But I don't think I'm overly critical. I just try to focus on moving forward and getting things sorted out. \n\nI hope that's the kind of answer you were looking for? I'm just trying to be as accurate as I can without making it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your relationships with friends or family due to your worries about retirement and the dental practice?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, definitely not. My friendships and my marriage are perfectly fine. I try very hard to keep things pleasant for everyone around me. That's a good sign, isn't it, Doctor? I've postponed a few parts of the retirement handoff. Well, um... I really wouldn't say "significant difficulties." That sounds like things are falling apart, and I promise you they aren't. I'm keeping everything afloat, really I am. My wife and I are doing fine, and my staff hasn't complained.

I just keep second-guessing my decisions, wanting to ensure the new owners have a flawless transition.
